The remaining 2 per cent identified as adherents of other religions, including the <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Adherents2001_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adherents2001-162">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-census2012_158-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census2012-158">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> The main Christian denominations are <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a> and other <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Roman Catholicism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Education_in_the_Falkland_Islands" title="Education in the Falkland Islands">Education in the Falkland Islands</a>, which follows <a href="/wiki/Education_in_England" title="Education in England">England's system</a>, is free and compulsory for residents aged between 5 and 16 years.<sup id="cite_ref-EDU_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EDU-166">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Primary education is available at Stanley, RAF Mount Pleasant (for children of service personnel) and a number of rural settlements. Secondary education is only available in Stanley, which offers boarding facilities and 12 subjects to <a href="/wiki/General_Certificate_of_Secondary_Education" title="General Certificate of Secondary Education">General Certificate of Secondary Education</a> (GCSE) level. Students aged 16 or older may study at colleges in England for their <a href="/wiki/GCE_Advanced_Level" class="mw-redirect" title="GCE Advanced Level">GCE Advanced Level</a> or vocational qualifications. The Falkland Islands government pays for older students to attend institutions of higher education, usually in the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-EDU_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EDU-166">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_Falkland_Islands" title="Culture of the Falkland Islands">Culture of the Falkland Islands</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hope-Place.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Two men in front of a fireplace about to exchange a drink" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Hope-Place.jpg/220px-Hope-Place.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="127" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Hope-Place.jpg/330px-Hope-Place.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Hope-Place.jpg/440px-Hope-Place.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2049" data-file-height="1187" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hope-Place.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Gaucho" title="Gaucho">Gauchos</a> from mainland South America, such as these two men having <a href="/wiki/Mate_(beverage)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mate (beverage)">mate</a> at Hope Place in East Falkland, influenced the local dialect.</div></div></div>
<p>Falklands culture is based on the <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Culture of the United Kingdom">cultural traditions</a> of its British settlers but has also been influenced by <a href="/wiki/Hispanic_America" title="Hispanic America">Hispanic South America</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139-157">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Falklanders still use some terms and place names from the former Gaucho inhabitants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200121_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200121-167">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The Falklands' predominant and official language is English, with the foremost dialect being <a href="/wiki/British_English" title="British English">British English</a>; nonetheless, some inhabitants also speak Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139-157">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> According to naturalist <a href="/wiki/Will_Wagstaff" title="Will Wagstaff">Will Wagstaff</a>, "the Falkland Islands are a very social place, and stopping for a chat is a way of life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200121_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200121-167">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The islands have one weekly newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/Penguin_News" title="Penguin News">The Penguin News</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200166_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200166-168">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> and television and radio broadcasts generally feature programming from the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139_157-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinahan2013139-157">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Wagstaff describes local cuisine as "very British in character with much use made of the homegrown vegetables, local lamb, mutton, beef, and fish". Common between meals are "home made cakes and biscuits with tea or coffee".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200163–64_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200163–64-169">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Social activities are, according to Wagstaff, "typical of that of a small British town with a variety of clubs and organisations covering many aspects of community life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200165_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagstaff200165-170">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to researcher Simon Taylor, the exact Gaelic etymology is unclear as the "falk" in the name could have stood for "hidden" (<i>falach</i>), "wash" (<i>failc</i>), or "heavy rain" (<i>falc</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylorMárkus2005158_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylorMárkus2005158-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-24"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-24">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Based on his analysis of Falkland Islands discovery claims, historian <a href="/wiki/John_Dunmore" title="John Dunmore">John Dunmore</a> concludes that "[a] number of countries could therefore lay some claim to the archipelago under the heading of first discoverers: Spain, Holland, Britain, and even Italy and Portugal&#160;– although the last two claimants might be stretching things a little."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDunmore200593_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunmore200593-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-28"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-28">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In 1764, Bougainville claimed the islands in the name of <a href="/wiki/Louis_XV_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XV of France">Louis XV of France</a>. In 1765, British captain John Byron claimed the islands in the name of <a href="/wiki/George_III_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George III of the United Kingdom">George III of Great Britain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGustafson19889–10_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGustafson19889–10-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDunmore2005139–40_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunmore2005139–40-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-36"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-36">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to Argentine legal analyst Roberto Laver, the United Kingdom disregards Jewett's actions because the government he represented "was not recognized either by Britain or any other foreign power at the time" and "no act of occupation followed the ceremony of claiming possession".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaver200173_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaver200173-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Before leaving for the Falklands Vernet stamped his grant at the British Consulate, repeating this when Buenos Aires extended his grant in 1828.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECawkell200148–50_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECawkell200148–50-37">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The cordial relationship between the consulate and Vernet led him to express "the wish that, in the event of the British returning to the islands, <a href="/wiki/Government_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Government of the United Kingdom">HMG</a> would take his settlement under their protection".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECawkell200150_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECawkell200150-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-43"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-43">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The log of the <i>"Lexington"</i> only reports the destruction of arms and a powder store, but Vernet made a claim for compensation from the US Government stating that the entire settlement was destroyed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPascoePepper2008541–44_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoePepper2008541–44-42">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-49"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-49">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">As discussed by Roberto Laver, not only did Rosas not break relations with Britain because of the "essential" nature of "British economic support", but he offered the Falklands "as a bargaining chip&#160;... in exchange for the cancellation of Argentina's million-pound debt with the British bank of <a href="/wiki/Baring_Brothers" class="mw-redirect" title="Baring Brothers">Baring Brothers</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaver2001122–23_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaver2001122–23-47">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In 1850, Rosas' government ratified the <a href="/wiki/Arana%E2%80%93Southern_Treaty" title="Arana–Southern Treaty">Arana–Southern Treaty</a>, which put "an end to the existing differences, and of restoring perfect relations of friendship" between the United Kingdom and Argentina.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHertslet1851105_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHertslet1851105-48">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-52"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-52">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Argentina protested in 1841, 1849, 1884, 1888, 1908, 1927 and 1933, and has made annual protests to the United Nations since 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGustafson198834_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGustafson198834-51">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-60">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">There were continual tensions with the colonial administration over Lafone's failure to establish any permanent settlers, and over the price of beef supplied to the settlement. Moreover, although his concession required Lafone to bring settlers from the UK, most of the settlers he brought were gauchos from Uruguay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrange198784_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrange198784-59">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-75">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The minefields were fenced off and marked; there remain unexploded ordnance and improvised explosive devices.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_Mines_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_Mines-73">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Detection and clearance of mines in the Falklands has proven difficult as some were air-delivered and not in marked fields; approximately 80% lie in sand or peat, where the position of mines can shift, making removal procedures difficult.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-78">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In 1976, Lord Shackleton produced a report into the economic future of the islands; but his recommendations were not implemented because Britain sought to avoid confronting Argentina over sovereignty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECawkell2001147_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECawkell2001147-77">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Lord Shackleton was once again tasked, in 1982, to produce a report into the economic development of the islands. His new report criticised the large farming companies, and recommended transferring ownership of farms from absentee landlords to local landowners. Shackleton also suggested diversifying the economy into fishing, oil exploration, and tourism; moreover, he recommended the establishment of a road network, and conservation measures to preserve the islands' natural resources.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECawkell2001147_77-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECawkell2001147-77">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-108"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-108">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Argentina considers that, in 1833, the UK established an "illegal occupation" of the Falklands after expelling Argentine authorities and settlers from the islands with a threat of "greater force" and, afterwards, barring Argentines from resettling the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-RIS_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RIS-102">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto1_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-103">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_104-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-104">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> The Falkland Islands' government considers that only Argentina's military personnel was expelled in 1833, but its civilian settlers were "invited to stay" and did so except for 2 and their wives.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> International affairs scholar Lowell Gustafson considers that "[t]he use of force by the British on the Falkland Islands in 1833 was less dramatic than later Argentine rhetoric has suggested".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGustafson198826_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGustafson198826-107">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-161"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-161">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">At the time of the 2012 census, 91 Falklands residents were overseas.<sup id="cite_ref-census2012_158-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census2012-158">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div>
